Look at the debug output:\n\n```\nMODIFIED: tag={...}t, attr=text, id=281473761709056, new_val=보고일: 2026-11-20\nMODIFIED: tag={...}t, attr=text, id=281473761709376, new_val=작성자: 정도현\nMODIFIED: tag={...}t, attr=text, id=281473761708992, new_val=부서: 물류관리팀\n...\nCHECKING mod_id=281473761709056, tag=lineseg\n```\n\nWait! `mod_id=281473761709056` was the id of a `<hp:t>` element when we modified it, but when we look it up later in `id_to_elem`, it's now `tag=lineseg`! This means the `id()` values are being REUSED for different elements!\n\nThis is because `id()` in Python returns the memory address of the object. When the old `<hp:t>` element is modified, it might still exist in the tree, but the same memory address could be reused for newly created elements during tree traversal or serialization. Actually, more likely: the issue is that `root_elem.iter()` creates new iterator objects, and elements might be garbage collected and recreated, or the `id()` values are reused after elements are garbage collected.\n\nWait, that's not quite right either. Elements in lxml are C objects and their Python wrapper's `id()` should be stable... unless there's some issue with how lxml handles element identity.\n\nBut looking more carefully:\n- Modified element with `id=281473761709056` was a `<hp:t>` \n- When checking with `id_to_elem.get(mod_id)`, it returns an element with `tag=lineseg`\n\nActually, the `id_to_elem` dictionary is built AFTER all modifications, using `{id(e): e for e in root_elem.iter()}`. The `root_elem.iter()` was called on the modified tree. The original `<hp:t>` element with that id might have been garbage collected, and a new element (`lineseg`) got the same memory address.\n\nThis is a fundamental issue with using `id()` for element identity! `id()` in Python can be reused when objects are garbage collected.\n\nThe fix is simple: instead of storing `id(elem)` in `modified_paragraphs`, we should store the element itself (or a more stable identifier).
